● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · New backend's API contract — how much can it change? → i didnt understand
     what ur asking

● Fair — let me explain plainly with a real example from your code.

  What "API contract" means: the exact list of URLs the frontend calls, and the
  exact shape of the JSON going each way. Right now
  printvendo-web/lib/flowApi.ts calls POST /wallet/hold, then has to also call
  POST /printers/{id}/print. Two calls for one user action ("pay with wallet").
  If the second call fails, the student paid and nothing prints. That's an
  ambiguity baked into the contract, not into the code.

  The question is: when we build the new backend, do we copy those URLs and
  shapes exactly as they are today, or do we redesign them?

  - If we copy them exactly, nothing in printvendo-web or printvendo-owner needs
  touching. Point them at the new server, done. But the new backend inherits
  the same confusing shapes — wallet pay is still two calls, /owner/* still
  means admin, and so on. We'd have cleaned the insides but not the ambiguity 
  you're complaining about.
  - If we redesign them (e.g. one endpoint POST /orders/{id}/pay that does the
  whole thing), the confusion disappears. The cost: in printvendo-web the files
  lib/api.ts, lib/flowApi.ts, lib/queries.ts, lib/batches.ts get rewired, and in
  printvendo-owner just lib/api.ts. No page, component, or design file changes
  — those apps deliberately isolate all backend talk into lib/. So the apps look
  and behave identically to a user; only the plumbing underneath moves.

  My recommendation is redesign, because "clear all the ambiguity" is the whole
  point of this exercise, and the frontend cost is genuinely 5 files.

● kiosk_type already exists as PLATFORM | SOLD | SAAS on Printer — matches your
  table exactly. But that's where I found the sharpest ambiguity in the system:

  kiosk_type declares the business relationship. It does not decide where the 
  money goes. gateway_routing.resolves_to_owner_gateway() ignores kiosk_type
  entirely and derives routing from (active subscription AND configured Razorpay
  keys). So a SOLD kiosk — a shop that bought hardware and takes their own
  money — silently starts routing student payments into your Razorpay the moment
  their subscription lapses or their keys are cleared.

  That is either a deliberate lever or a money bug. Need your answer before I
  model it.

● Root cause first, because it decides the answer.

  Today's routers are organised by audience, not by subject. kiosk.py (2059
  lines) is "everything a kiosk owner can do": paper, pricing, jobs, analytics,
  subscriptions, invoices, staff, restarts. owner.py (1883) is "everything an
  admin can do" — over the same subjects. refiller.py is paper again. admin.py
  is paper reset again.

  So paper is implemented four times, refunds twice, pricing twice, clear-queue 
  twice. Your own audit caught three of those. That's not sloppiness, it's the
  inevitable output of that layout — and it's exactly where drift becomes a
  money bug (the wallet leak came from gateway routing being re-derived in two
  places).

  Fix: organise by subject, and make audience a permission check on top. Three
  ways to do that.

  ---
  A. Modular monolith, one module per bounded context ← recommended

  app/
    core/          config, db, security, errors, money, ids
    modules/
      identity/    users, roles, sessions, google/guest login
      kiosks/      printer registry, kiosk_type, onboarding, paper, pricing,
  staff
      printing/    jobs, files, pipeline (ghostscript/downsample), queue, pi hub
      payments/    razorpay, orders, refunds, gateway routing, fees
      wallet/      balance, ledger, topups, holds
      billing/     plans, subscriptions, invoices
      ops/         admin alerts, audit log, analytics, exports
    api/
      student/     routes for printvendo-web
      owner/       routes for printvendo-owner
      admin/       routes for the new console
      refiller/    routes for printit-refiller-app
      device/      routes + websocket for pi-agent

  Each module owns its tables and exposes a typed service API. Modules never
  import each other's ORM models — only each other's service functions. Enforced
  mechanically by import-linter in CI, so it can't rot the way the current code
  did.

  The api/ layer is thin: auth, validate, call one service, serialize. Five
  audiences, one implementation of paper. Deletes the four-copies problem by
  construction.

  Single process, single Postgres, single deploy. Redis added for the Pi
  WebSocket fan-out so you can finally run more than one uvicorn worker.

  Cost: the module-boundary discipline is a real constraint — some things that
  are one function today become a service call across a boundary.

  ---
  B. Classic layered (routers / services / repositories / models)

  Keep today's shape, add a proper service layer and repositories underneath so
  logic lives in one place.

  Cheaper to build, very familiar. But nothing bounds a layer —
  services/kiosk_service.py grows to 2000 lines exactly like kiosk.py did,
  because the same pressure is still there and no rule stops it. This is how you
  got here.

  ---
  C. Split services (API + print/WS service + payments service)

  True isolation, and the WebSocket hub scales independently.

  Wrong at your size. A payment and a print job must commit together; across
  services that's a distributed transaction or an outbox, and you'd be running
  three deploys, three log streams and a message bus solo. Revisit at 100+
  kiosks.

  ---
  Recommend A. It's the only one that structurally prevents the specific failure
  you're rewriting to escape, and it costs about the same as B to build.

● Section 1 — Domain model & vocabulary

  Read the core models. The triangle is the problem: Job (uploaded doc),
  PrinterJob (dispatch), Payment (money) each independently point at user +
  printer, and nothing owns the transaction. That's the direct cause of your
  worst hazard — /wallet/hold marks a Payment PAID but creates no PrinterJob, so
  the caller must remember a second POST /printers/{id}/print or the student
  paid for nothing. /wallet/hold/multi exists purely because Payment.job_id is
  NOT NULL and can't span 3 files.

  Fix: introduce Order as the aggregate. One student, one kiosk, N documents,
  one payment, N print tasks — created in one transaction. The two-call hazard
  and the /multi duplicate both disappear by construction.

  Today: —
  New: Order
  Why: The missing aggregate. Pay and enqueue commit together or not at all.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: Job
  New: Document
  Why: It's an uploaded file + options + page count. It was never a "job".
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: PrinterJob
  New: PrintTask
  Why: One document dispatched to one device; owns queue state.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: Payment
  New: Payment
  Why: Now belongs to Order, not to a single document.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: Printer (30 cols)
  New: Kiosk + KioskDevice + KioskPaper
  Why: One table is doing business unit + Pi device identity + pricing + paper +

  email-nag state. Split by who changes it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: printer_owners + printer_refillers
  New: KioskAssignment(kiosk, user, role)
  Why: Two identical tables. One does it.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: 4 booleans on User
  New: UserRole(user, role)
  Why: is_admin / is_kiosk_owner / is_refiller / is_guest / subscription_enabled

  as loose flags is how a refiller nearly reaches money data.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Today: Settlement, settlement_type
  New: deleted
  Why: Retired by your own docs.

  One more thing, and it kills a whole bug class: today printer_id is both a
  public string and a numeric PK, used inconsistently — printvendo-web/CLAUDE.md
  has a written warning about it. New rule: every id crossing the API is an 
  opaque prefixed string (ksk_7f3a, ord_91bc, doc_44de). Numeric PKs never leave
  the database. No caller can ever pass the wrong one again.

  Also fixing in passing: Job.price_cents is Numeric(10,2) holding rupees. The
  name is a lie. It becomes amount_inr.

  Does the domain model look right so far?

● Section 2 — Workflows & state machines

  The one gate. Today three services independently re-derive "whose Razorpay
  collects here": gateway_routing, wallet_eligibility, refunds. Two of them
  drifting is what caused the wallet money leak. New backend has exactly one
  function:

  kiosk_payment_gate(kiosk) -> OWNER_GATEWAY | PLATFORM_GATEWAY | CLOSED

  Every consumer — order pricing, wallet eligibility, refund destination, kiosk
  listing — calls it. Nothing re-derives it. Your lapse rule lives here:
  SOLD/SAAS with no active subscription or no keys returns CLOSED, and a CLOSED
  kiosk is not listed and cannot take an order.

  Lifecycles

  Document   UPLOADED → PROCESSING → READY
                            ↓            ↓
                        REJECTED      EXPIRED (file purged by retention)

  Order      DRAFT → AWAITING_PAYMENT → PAID → DISPATCHED → COMPLETED
                            ↓             ↓                     ↓
                         EXPIRED      REFUNDED           PARTIALLY_FAILED

  PrintTask  QUEUED → SENT_TO_DEVICE → PRINTING → PRINTED
                            ↓              ↓          ↓
                         BLOCKED        FAILED    (retention purge)

  Kiosk      REGISTERED → APPROVED → CONFIGURED → LIVE ⇄ MAINTENANCE
                                                    ↓
                                              SUSPENDED_BILLING → LIVE
                                                    ↓
                                                 RETIRED

  CONFIGURED is auto-skipped for PLATFORM kiosks (platform keys already exist).
  SUSPENDED_BILLING is entered automatically the moment the gate returns CLOSED
  — that is your lapse rule, visible as state rather than as an emergent side
  effect.

  Student print flow — collapses today's 2–3 calls into one committed
  transaction:

  pick kiosk → upload docs (server prices them) → POST /orders
    → server: reserve paper, compute price from KIOSK pricing, open Razorpay
  order
    → pay (wallet OR gateway)
    → ONE transaction: Payment=PAID, Order=PAID, PrintTasks created, WS push to
  device

  Wallet and gateway are two branches into the same commit. There is no path
  where money moves and tasks don't exist.

  Other flows: wallet top-up (order → webhook → ledger credit, idempotent on
  razorpay_payment_id); refund (destination forced by the gate — owner-collected
  can only go back-to-source); paper refill (refiller or owner resets sheets,
  clears nag state, kiosk leaves out-of-paper); subscription (plan → order →
  webhook → ACTIVE, free_until for your N free months, expiry → grace → gate
  CLOSED); Pi device (register → token → WS connect → heartbeat → jobs).

  ---
  One finding I need your call on. payments.py:787 adds min(2% of base, ₹2) as a
  "platform fee", then hands the entire amount to whichever gateway the routing
  picked. At a SOLD/SAAS kiosk that's the owner's Razorpay — so the platform 
  fee is collected by the owner. You never receive it. It's a fee named after
  you that funds someone else.

● Noted — fee stays on every gateway payment, owner keeps it at owner-gateway 
  kiosks. Recording it as a deliberate commercial decision in the spec so nobody
  "fixes" it later.

  Section 3 — API contract

  Five audiences, five prefixes, honest names. /owner finally means owner; admin
  is /admin.

  /v1/app/*        printvendo-web
  /v1/owner/*      printvendo-owner
  /v1/admin/*      new admin console
  /v1/refiller/*   printit-refiller-app
  /v1/device/*     pi-agent  (+ /v1/device/ws)

  Student — the flow that mattered:

  POST /v1/app/documents            upload → {id, pages, amount_inr}
  POST /v1/app/orders               {kiosk_id, document_ids[]} → {id, total,
  fee, payable}
  POST /v1/app/orders/{id}/pay      {method: wallet|gateway}
  POST /v1/app/orders/{id}/pay/verify   (gateway only)

  pay with method=wallet is one call that commits everything — debit, order
  PAID, print tasks created, WebSocket push. The /wallet/hold +
  /printers/{id}/print two-step and the separate /wallet/hold/multi are both
  gone.

  Rest of student surface: /auth/* (register, login, google, guest, refresh,
  logout, me), /kiosks + /kiosks/{id} + favorites, /documents/analyze,
  /documents/photo-layout, /orders history + /orders/{id}/invoice(.pdf), /wallet
  + /wallet/ledger + /wallet/topup, /shop/items, /push/*.

  Paper-shop purchases stop being a fake print job — they're an Order with a
  shop line item.

  Owner: /me, /kiosks, /kiosks/{id},
  /kiosks/{id}/pricing|paper|status|queue|device, /kiosks/{id}/orders (+
  force-complete, refund), /earnings, /analytics, /orders/export, /subscriptions
  (+ invoice), /payment-config, /bank-details, /staff, /refill-logs.

  Refiller: /me, /kiosks, /kiosks/{id}/paper, /kiosks/{id}/refill-logs. Paper
  only, no money — enforced by role, not by a separate copy of the code.

  Device: /register, /heartbeat, /health, /tasks/next, /tasks/{id}/file,
  /tasks/{id}/status, /agent/version, /update-status, /ws. Ends today's split
  across printers.py and pi.py.

  ~150 routes → ~85. Not from removing features — from paper existing once
  instead of four times, refunds once instead of twice, pricing once instead of
  twice.

  Contract rules that are load-bearing:
  - Errors keep {"detail": "<human sentence>"}. printvendo-owner/lib/api.ts
  surfaces detail verbatim on purpose, and those sentences are written for
  humans. Not replacing them with codes.
  - Idempotency-Key required on every money-moving POST.
  - CORS allowlist from env, not hardcoded in main.py.
  - One Razorpay webhook endpoint, not three. One URL to register.
  - Owner/refiller responses carry no student identity — same rule as today, but
  enforced in one serializer instead of trusted per-endpoint.

  Contract look right?

● Section 4 — Security & authorization

  Your rule — owner controls only their own kiosks, admin controls all — is the
  current intent, but it's enforced by remembering to call a helper. Two places
  where that already failed:

  1. Owner Razorpay secrets are stored in plaintext.
  KioskPaymentConfig.razorpay_key_secret is a plain String column. Its comment
  says "stored encrypted/hashed" — but grep -rn "encrypt|Fernet|cryptography" 
  app/ returns only that comment. There is no encryption code in the backend. A
  dump of your Postgres hands over every kiosk owner's live payment-gateway
  credentials. This matters right now, because the migration plan starts with
  pg_dump.

  2. Staff assignment is cross-tenant. POST /kiosk/printers/{id}/staff/{user_id}
  (kiosk.py:911) checks that you own the kiosk and that the target is a 
  refiller — but never that the refiller has anything to do with you. So an
  owner can:
  - enumerate the whole user table by response code (404 = no user, 400 = user
  exists but not refiller, 200 = user exists and is a refiller), and
  - bind another shop's refiller to their kiosk, after which GET /kiosk/staff
  returns that person's email and full name.

  Owner A harvests owner B's staff identity. This is the same class of bug your
  own owner.py:44 warning is about.

  The new design makes it structural, not remembered.

  Every kiosk-scoped query goes through one resolver:

  scope = kiosk_scope(actor)        # OWNER → their assignments
                                    # REFILLER → their assignments
                                    # ADMIN → all
  repo.kiosks(scope)                # no unscoped query exists in route code

  Route handlers cannot reach an unscoped repository — enforced by
  import-linter, same mechanism as module boundaries. Admin isn't a bypass path
  with its own unchecked router (today's /owner/*); admin is the same resolver 
  with a wider scope. That deletes the  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ continue                                                                      

● ...that deletes the "DO NOT LOOSEN" hazard entirely — there is no second,
  unchecked path to loosen.

  Rest of the security model:

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Area     │                           Rule                            │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Owner Razorpay secrets encrypted with an app-held key     │
  │ Secrets at  │ (envelope encryption, key from env/KMS). Never returned   │
  │ rest        │ by any API — masked to rzp_live_••••4821. Migration       │
  │             │ decrypts nothing; it re-encrypts on load.                 │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Short access JWT (15 min), rotating refresh with reuse    │
  │ Tokens      │ detection — a replayed refresh revokes the whole family.  │
  │             │ Refresh tokens already hashed today; keeping that.        │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Per-device token, hashed at rest (as today), plus         │
  │ Device auth │ rotation endpoint and revoke-on-unassign. Device tokens   │
  │             │ scope to one kiosk, nothing else.                         │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ documents/{id}/file is owner-only. The Pi never gets a    │
  │ File access │ bearer path — it gets a short-lived signed URL bound to   │
  │             │ its PrintTask. Today's /pi/jobs/{id}/file is              │
  │             │ device-token-wide.                                        │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ PDF         │ Ghostscript is an RCE surface. Runs with -dSAFER, no      │
  │ pipeline    │ shell, hard timeout, page/size caps, in a subprocess with │
  │             │  a scratch dir it cannot escape.                          │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Webhooks    │ One endpoint, signature verified, replay-blocked by the   │
  │             │ existing unique constraint on razorpay_payment_id.        │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ Owner/refiller/admin-of-other-scope responses go through  │
  │ Student PII │ a serializer that has no name/email/user_id field at all. │
  │             │  Can't leak what isn't in the type.                       │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Audit       │ Every mutation by owner/refiller/admin recorded with      │
  │             │ actor, scope, before/after.                               │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Rate limits │ On auth, upload, order creation, and payment verify —     │
  │             │ real client IP via proxy headers.                         │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Enumeration │ Staff/user lookup never returns "exists but wrong role".  │
  │             │ One 404 for everything.                                   │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

● Section 5 — Production data migration

  Checked how to reconstruct the missing Order from legacy rows, because that's
  the one non-mechanical part. Good news: it's derivable.

  /wallet/hold/multi writes one ledger row and N Payment rows that all share
  razorpay_order_id = "WALLET:{ledger_id}" (wallet.py:598-632). Gateway payments
  share a real Razorpay order id. So:

  ▎ razorpay_order_id is the grouping key. Every set of legacy payments sharing 
  ▎ one becomes exactly one new Order. Payments with no shared id become 
  ▎ single-document orders. No guessing.

  Mechanics

  pg_dump prod → restore to a local printit_legacy database, read-only.
  Migration lives in migration/ in the new repo, reads legacy through raw SQL 
  only — the legacy ORM models never enter the new codebase, or the old shapes
  come back in through the side door.

  Every new table keeps a nullable indexed legacy_id. That makes the whole run
  idempotent and re-runnable: a second pass updates instead of duplicating. Kept
  permanently, because when a number looks wrong six months from now you'll
  want to trace it back.

  Table map

  ┌────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │         Legacy         │         New         │          Note           │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ users                  │ users + user_roles  │ 4 booleans → role rows  │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                        │ kiosks +            │                         │
  │ printers               │ kiosk_devices +     │ one table → three       │
  │                        │ kiosk_paper         │                         │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ printer_owners +       │ kiosk_assignments   │ two → one               │
  │ printer_refillers      │                     │                         │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                        │                     │ price_cents →           │
  │ jobs                   │ documents           │ amount_inr (already     │
  │                        │                     │ rupees)                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ payments               │ orders + payments   │ grouped by              │
  │                        │                     │ razorpay_order_id       │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ printer_jobs           │ print_tasks         │                         │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ wallets, wallet_ledger │ same                │ near 1:1, balances      │
  │                        │                     │ checksummed             │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ subscriptions          │ subscriptions       │ settlement_type dropped │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ kiosk_payment_configs  │ same, encrypted     │ plaintext secrets       │
  │                        │                     │ encrypted on load       │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ notifications          │ admin_alerts        │ renamed to what it is   │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ admin_audit_log        │ audit_log           │                         │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │                        │                     │ exported to CSV and     │
  │ settlements            │ not migrated        │ archived — retired, but │
  │                        │                     │  it's financial history │
  ├────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ refresh_tokens         │ not migrated        │ everyone re-logs-in.    │
  │                        │                     │ Free security reset.    │
  └────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  Cleaning rules — nothing is deleted silently. Anything dropped goes to a
  quarantine table with a reason, and the run prints a reconciliation report:
  row counts in/out/quarantined per table, plus wallet-balance and revenue
  totals that must match prod exactly or the run fails.

  - Orphans (job with missing user, printer_job with missing printer) →
  quarantine, not delete
  - Guest accounts with zero orders → quarantine
  - Documents whose files retention already purged → row kept, marked EXPIRED,
  no file
  - Dangling/duplicate Razorpay ids → quarantine
  - Kiosks whose kiosk_type is still the PLATFORM default but that have an owner
  → flagged for your manual review before load. This is the one thing I won't
  auto-decide: getting it wrong sends money to the wrong Razorpay.

  Files (storage/original|converted|templates) copy across by path, verified by
  count and size against the DB rows that reference them.

  Section 5 OK? Then I'll do deploy/cutover + testing, and write the spec.

✻ Sautéed for 1m 49s

❯ proceed                                                                       

● Section 6 — Testing, deploy, cutover

  Testing. TDD throughout, but two suites carry the real weight:

  1. Authorization matrix — generated, not handwritten. Every route × every role
  × (own scope / other scope / no scope) → expected status. Generated from the
  route table, so a new route with no matrix entry fails the build. That's what
  turns "owner controls only their own kiosks" from a convention into something
  that cannot regress.
  2. Money invariants as property tests. Wallet balance never negative;
  sum(ledger) == balance for every user; order.total == sum(document amounts) + 
  fee; refunds never exceed captured; every payment resolves to exactly one
  gateway. Run against generated histories, not just examples.

  Plus: contract fixtures per audience, migration reconciliation run against a
  real prod snapshot, Razorpay test-mode + webhook replay, device/WebSocket
  integration.

  Deploy. New repo/directory, side by side with prod. Docker Compose: Postgres +
  Redis + API + Caddy. Redis moves the Pi WebSocket registry out of process, so
  --workers 1 finally goes away. Alembic migrations run as a pre-start job.
  Staging domain (e.g. api2.innvera.online) so the new stack is fully live and
  testable while production keeps serving.

  Cutover: snapshot → migrate → verify → point dev builds of both apps at
  staging → exercise → brief read-only freeze on prod → incremental delta
  migration (upsert by legacy_id) → flip. Prod stays untouched and rollback-able
  the whole time.

  ---
  One decision left, and it's the highest-risk one in the plan: the Pi agents.

  Every other client is a web app — I redeploy it and it's done. A Pi agent is a
  physical box in a shop. If a rollout goes wrong there's no remote hands, and
  a bricked agent is a dead kiosk earning nothing.

  There is an auto-update mechanism (agent_version, POST 
  /pi/{id}/trigger-update, /update-status), so pushing new agent code is
  possible. But it's the one part of the cutover that can fail physically.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · The pi-agent device contract — redesign it like the rest, or freeze it? →
     Redesign it, roll agents out first

● Agents first, then backend. That works, but it forces one design consequence I
  want explicit:

  The new agent must be dual-protocol. You're rolling agents out while
  production is still live and still speaking the old protocol — so the new
  agent speaks old to prod, and flips to /v1/device/* by remote config at
  cutover. The dual-protocol burden moves from the backend to the agent, where
  it's a config switch rather than two live surfaces.

  Mitigations baked into the plan: canary → 10% → fleet staged rollout; previous
  version kept on disk with a watchdog that reverts if heartbeat fails for N
  minutes; cutover gated on 100% of kiosks reporting the new agent_version.

  Writing the spec now.

● Self-reviewing the spec caught a real error in my own cutover sequence. Fixing
  it.

  The bug: I had "roll agents incrementally" and "flip DNS" in the same plan.
  Those contradict. If the API domain repoints, every not-yet-migrated agent
  hits the new backend speaking the old protocol and dies. And if kiosks move
  over before the freeze, they'd take orders into the new DB while students are
  still ordering into the old one — two live ledgers.

  Students and kiosks have to move together, in one window.

● Spec written: docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-14-new-cloud-backend-design.md.
  Not committed — repo root isn't a git repo (only cloud-backend/,
  printvendo-web/, printvendo-owner/ are).

  On new agent code — not yet, and here's the concrete reason.

  The agent is a client of /v1/device/*. That server module doesn't exist yet.
  Writing the agent first means writing it against an imagined API, then
  rewriting it once the real one lands — and the agent is the one component
  where being wrong costs a physical site visit.

  Correct order, now that you have SSH (which removes the rollout risk
  entirely):

  1. printing module + device API + WebSocket hub on the new backend
  2. Agent written against it, tested against staging on a canary kiosk with a
  real printer
  3. Staged inert on every kiosk over SSH
  4. Flipped during the cutover window

  Your SSH access already earned its keep in the design: the auto-update
  mechanism is off the critical path, rollback is a one-line SSH revert, and the
  new agent gets staged in advance so cutover is a restart, not a deploy.

  Spec covers: why the rewrite (audience-vs-subject root cause), 12 recorded
  decisions, module architecture, domain model with Order as the missing
  aggregate, workflows + state machines, security model with the two live
  findings, full API contract, migration with the razorpay_order_id grouping
  key, testing strategy, cutover, frontend work, and a 9-part decomposition.

  Two findings in there worth reading first — both are live in production right
  now:
  - Owner Razorpay secrets are plaintext in Postgres (§6, Finding 1)
  - Owner staff-assign allows cross-tenant user enumeration + harvesting another
  shop's refiller's name and email (§6, Finding 2)

  Neither is caused by the rewrite; both exist today. Say the word and I'll
  patch them in the current backend separately, since the new one is months out.

  Review the spec and tell me what to change. Once you approve it I'll write the
  implementation plan.

● Plan complete and saved to
  docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-14-backend-foundation.md.

  15 tasks, TDD throughout: project skeleton + dev stack → money → ids → config
  → errors → crypto → security → db → app factory → Alembic → production image →
  import-linter → authz matrix harness → CI → docs.

  Self-review caught eight defects in my own draft, all fixed:

  1. Ordering bug — the database task needed Postgres, but the dev stack was
  Task 11. Moved into Task 1.
  2. Weak test — test_session_scope_rolls_back_on_error never raised an error.
  Rewritten to actually prove rollback.
  3. Windows subprocess — the Alembic test blanked os.environ, which stops
  Python launching on Windows. Now inherits and overrides one key.
  4. Shell mismatch — a VAR=x cmd line that fails in PowerShell. Both forms
  given.
  5. Wrong invocation — python -m importlinter.cli isn't an entry point. Uses
  the lint-imports console script.
  6. Import at file bottom in the authz test, used by a test above it. Moved to
  top.
  7. Forward reference to a task that hadn't run yet.
  8. Wrong expected count after adding tests.

  Two things in there worth knowing about:

  Task 13 builds the trap before there's anything to catch. The authz matrix
  harness fails the build on any route without a declared audience — and Step 5
  makes you add a throwaway route to watch it fail, then delete it. Built now,
  while /health is the only route, so every route from here on is forced through
  it. That's the mechanism the old backend lacked, which is why /owner/* ended
  up with a "DO NOT LOOSEN" comment instead of a check.

  Task 12 writes the import contracts while modules/ is empty. Nothing can
  violate them yet, so the first violation fails CI rather than becoming
  precedent.

  Two execution options:

  1. Subagent-Driven (recommended) — fresh subagent per task, I review between
  tasks, fast iteration
  2. Inline Execution — tasks run in this session with checkpoints for review

  Which approach?

● Run this — it'll prompt for your postgres superuser password interactively:

  printvendo LOGIN PASSWORD 'printvendo';" -c "CREATE DATABASE printvendo OWNER
  printvendo;" -c "CREATE DATABASE printvendo_test OWNER printvendo;"

  in staging or production, which read DATABASE_URL from env.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
● No saved credentials anywhere, so the password needs resetting. Let me read
  the auth config to write you an exact script.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Only loopback rules — nothing exposed externally. That makes the standard
  recovery safe.

  What this does, plainly: it temporarily sets Postgres to trust local
  connections (no password), creates the printvendo role and databases, sets a
  new known password for the postgres superuser, then restores the original auth
  config and restarts. The passwordless window is a few seconds and applies
  only to 127.0.0.1/::1.

  It must run as Administrator — it writes into C:\Program 
  Files\PostgreSQL\18\data\ and restarts the service.

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +100 (ctrl+o to expand)

● Run this — it triggers a UAC prompt and opens an elevated window that stays
  open so you can read the output:

  Start-Process powershell -Verb RunAs -ArgumentList
  '-NoExit','-ExecutionPolicy','Bypass','-File','C:\Users\gurua\AppData\Local\Te
  mp\claude\C--Users-gurua-Downloads-Telegram-Desktop-printit-upgrade\011beb0f-e
  517-44bf-bcc5-36021a0fbf2e\scratchpad\reset-pg.ps1'

  The restore of pg_hba.conf is in a finally block, so auth goes back to
  scram-sha-256 even if something in the middle fails. It ends by connecting as
  printvendo with the real password to prove it worked. New postgres password
  will be postgres_local_dev — write it down.

● Task 2 done (b161ade) — 9 tests pass, and the implementer verified the
  ROUND_HALF_UP test actually bites by removing the argument and watching it
  fail. Good discipline.

  The Postgres script failed, and I know why. First, confirming your database is
  back up.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● Your database is fine — service RUNNING, config correctly restored, no 
  corruption. The finally block did exactly its job.

  Root cause: Set-Content -Encoding utf8 in Windows PowerShell 5.1 writes a
  UTF-8 BOM. Postgres can't parse pg_hba.conf with a BOM, so the service refused
  to start. The restored file has no BOM (starts with # P) because Copy-Item
  restores bytes verbatim.

  pg_hba.conf is pure ASCII, so writing it as ASCII avoids the BOM entirely.
  Fixing that and adding a start-verification so a failure is reported clearly
  instead of as a bare Restart-Service error.

  Made 1 scratchpad edit +18 -8 (ctrl+o to expand)

● Re-run the same command — the script is idempotent (it re-creates the backup,
  and skips objects that already exist):

  Start-Process powershell -Verb RunAs -ArgumentList
  '-NoExit','-ExecutionPolicy','Bypass','-File','C:\Users\gurua\AppData\Local\Te
  mp\claude\C--Users-gurua-Downloads-Telegram-Desktop-printit-upgrade\011beb0f-e
  517-44bf-bcc5-36021a0fbf2e\scratchpad\reset-pg.ps1'

  Continuing with Task 3 in parallel.

● Confirmed a real weakness in my plan: the tests still pass with 
  session.rollback() deleted, because session.close() discards the transaction
  anyway. That test was claiming to verify something it doesn't.

  Also test_cleanup isn't a test, and the rollback test depends on a table a
  previous test created — order-dependent and fragile. Fixing all three
  properly.

● Real design flaw in my plan, caught by the test. app/main.py ends with a
  module-level app = create_app(), which calls get_settings() at import time —
  so merely importing the module requires a fully populated environment. That
  breaks test collection, and would equally break Alembic and import-linter.

  Fix: drop the module-level instance and let uvicorn call the factory. No
  import-time environment dependency at all.

● Update(app\main.py)  ⎿  Added 8 lines
       4  the environment, and so a future worker process can build one w
          ith a different
       5  configuration. The CORS allowlist comes from settings — adding 
          a frontend is a
       6  deploy variable, never a code change.
       7 +                                                               
       8 +**There is deliberately no module-level `app = create_app()`.**
         + Building the app

         +ule would require                                              
      10 +a fully populated environment — which breaks pytest collection,
         + Alembic and

  SECRETS_ENCRYPTION_KEY.

  Two guardrails proven by deliberately breaking them — this was the point of
  the whole exercise:

  - Added import app.modules to app/core/money.py → exit 1, 2 contracts broken.
  Removed → exit 0. (Gotcha found: clear __pycache__ between runs or grimp
  reports stale bytecode — it briefly gave me a false result.)
  - Added an undeclared /temp-check route → build fails naming the route and
  telling the developer to declare its audience.

  Six defects in my own plan surfaced during implementation. All fixed, and
  recorded in the plan so the pattern is visible:

  now rewritten to assert the behavioural contract, with a docstring warning the

● Two findings that affect already-committed work, so fixing before writing the
  plan.

  1. Password hash scheme mismatch — would lock out every migrated user. The old
  backend hashes with pbkdf2_sha256 (explicitly, to dodge "platform-specific
  bcrypt issues"). My Task 7 used bcrypt only. Migrated users' hashes wouldn't
  verify and nobody could log in. Fix: accept both, treat pbkdf2 as deprecated
  so it silently upgrades to bcrypt on next successful login.

      58 +    """True when a verified hash uses a legacy scheme and shoul
         +d be upgraded.                                                 
      59 +

● Fixed and committed (9d7f6c7), suite at 74. That one would have been silent
  until cutover day — every pre-migration user unable to log in.

  Finding 2 is a genuine design conflict I need to resolve in the identity plan
  rather than steamroll:

  The old backend has a deliberate 60-second refresh grace window, with a
  comment explaining it fixed a real "logs out frequently" bug — two tabs
  refreshing concurrently, the loser gets told its token is invalid and the
  client bounces to /login. My spec says reuse detection revokes the whole token
  family. Implemented naively, that reintroduces the exact bug they fixed, and
  worse.

  Resolution: reuse inside the grace window is a concurrent refresh (allowed);
  reuse after it is a genuine replay (revoke the family). Keeps the UX fix and
  gains the security property.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ .registering should be with proper account , instead of using some random     
  mail                                                                          

● Worth separating two things, because they're already different in the code:

  POST /register already uses the real email the user types. It's
  EmailStr-validated and must be unique. No random address involved.

  The synthetic guest_<hex>@guest.printvendo is only for POST /guest — the
  "print without signing up" path. It exists because the users.email column is
  NOT NULL UNIQUE, so an anonymous row still needs something there, and it's
  deliberately unusable so nobody can ever log into it.

  So the real question is what happens when a guest later wants an account —
  right now they'd register fresh and silently lose their print history. The
  spec flagged that merge as "currently vague". That's a genuine gap.

● User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · What should happen with guest accounts and their synthetic email? → 3 and
     4
● Recorded: guests stay as-is (separate throwaway account, no merge), plus email
  verification on registration.

  Two consequences I'm deciding rather than asking, and stating plainly:

  - Unverified users can still log in. Blocking login on verification means a
  slow or bounced email locks someone out of an account they just paid to
  create. Verification status is exposed on /me, and gating specific actions
  (paying, printing) belongs to the modules that own those actions.
  - Google sign-in counts as verified — Google already proved the address.
  - Migrated production users are grandfathered as verified. They've been using
  the system for months; flipping them to unverified would gate your entire
  existing user base on a cutover night.

  Identity won't depend on an email provider — it returns the token and takes a
  Notifier protocol, so the Brevo adapter lands with the ops work.

● Verified: the finding is a false positive. Deactivated accounts already cannot
  authenticate — repository.py:23 filters is_active, and all three tests pass.

  But it has a fair point underneath: authenticate() reads as if it doesn't
  check, so if someone later relaxes get_by_email (say, to reactivate on login),
  this silently becomes exploitable. Making the invariant local and explicit —
  cheap, and it holds regardless of what the repository does.

● Found a serious bug in the authorisation harness itself. This version of
  FastAPI keeps include_router results as _IncludedRouter objects in app.routes
  rather than flattening them into APIRoutes — so my isinstance(route, APIRoute)
  filter skipped every route added via a router, which is how all real routes
  get added.

  The harness would have passed while checking nothing. The stale-entry test is
  what exposed it.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● original_router exposes the underlying APIRouter. Writing a proper recursive
  flattener — and pinning it with a test so this can't silently regress.

      29 +def _flatten(routes) -> list[APIRoute]:                        
      30 +    """Every APIRoute an app serves, however it was attached.  
      31 +                                                               
      32 +    This has to recurse. `include_router` does not splice the c
         +hild's routes                                                  
      33 +    into `app.routes`; it appends a wrapper object holding the 
         +original router.                                               
      34 +    Filtering `app.routes` for APIRoute instances therefore see
         +s only routes                                                  
      35 +    declared directly on the app and silently skips every mount
         +ed router --                                                   
      36 +    which is how all real routes are attached. That made this w
         +hole harness                                                   
      37 +    pass while checking nothing.                               
      38 +    """                                                        
      39 +    found: list[APIRoute] = []                                 
      40 +    for route in routes:                                       
      41 +        if isinstance(route, APIRoute):                        
      42 +            found.append(route)                                
      43 +            continue                                           
      44 +                                                               
      45 +        child = getattr(route, "original_router", None)        
      46 +        if child is not None and hasattr(child, "routes"):     
      47 +            found.extend(_flatten(child.routes))               
      48 +        elif hasattr(route, "routes"):                         
      49 +            found.extend(_flatten(route.routes))               
      50 +    return found

● The forbidden contract is catching indirect imports (api → repository →
  models), which isn't the rule I want — the API legitimately calls services
  that use models internally. The real rule is: no direct import of the ORM 
  module.

  That means identity should export its entity type properly, so the API has
  something legitimate to import.

      28 +def _configure_logging(settings: Settings) -> None:            
      29 +    """Make the application's own loggers audible.             
      30 +                                                               
      31 +    uvicorn configures its access and error loggers and nothing
         + else, so                                                      
      32 +    anything logged by app.* is discarded by the root logger's 
         +default WARNING                                                
      33 +    level. That silently broke LoggingNotifier: it claimed a de
         +veloper could                                                  
      34 +    finish an email-verification flow locally by reading the lo
         +g, and the line

      37 +    INFO in dev, WARNING elsewhere -- verification tokens are s
         +ecrets and have                                                
      38 +    no business in a production log.                           
      39 +    """                                                        
      40 +    level = logging.INFO if settings.ENV == "dev" else logging.
         +WARNING

      42 +    app_logger.setLevel(level)                                 
      43 +

      45 +        handler = logging.StreamHandler()                      
      46 +        handler.setFormatter(logging.Formatter("%(levelname)s %
         +(name)s: %(message)s"))                                        
      47 +        app_logger.addHandler(handler)

● Identity module complete. 209 tests, 5 import contracts, ruff clean, tree
  clean, 12 commits.

  Verified end-to-end against a real uvicorn process and real Postgres, not just
  TestClient: register → verification email → verify → login → refresh →
  logout, with a replayed verification token correctly refused.

  Your scope change landed: guests stay as they are, and registration now
  requires email verification. Three decisions I made while building it, all
  recorded in code where they'll be found:

  - Unverified users can still sign in — blocking login means a bounced email
  locks someone out
  - Google sign-in counts as verified
  - Migrated production users are grandfathered verified — this one is written
  into the migration's docstring, because the data migration depends on it.
  Getting it wrong gates your entire existing user base on cutover night.

  Seven defects found during implementation. The worst by a distance:

  ▎ The authorisation harness was checking nothing. This FastAPI version keeps 
  ▎ include_router results as wrapper objects rather than flattening them into 
  ▎ app.routes, so my isinstance(route, APIRoute) filter skipped every route 
  ▎ added via a router — which is how all real routes are added. It would have 
  ▎ reported green forever while protecting nothing. The stale-entry test caught
  ▎ it only because I declared the routes in the matrix before writing them.

  Also: logout set the cookie deletion on an injected Response then returned a
  different one (browser kept a revoked token and looked signed in);
  LoggingNotifier promised you could finish verification locally by reading the
  log, and uvicorn was swallowing every app log line.

  Four guardrails proven by deliberately breaking them — including the grace
  window: setting GRACE_SECONDS = 0 fails exactly one test, the concurrent
  refresh, reproducing the old backend's "logs out frequently" bug, and breaks
  nothing else. That's the evidence it fixes the UX bug without weakening replay
  detection.

  On the security review that fired mid-build: it flagged authenticate as an
  authentication bypass for deactivated accounts. That was a false positive —
  repo.get_by_email already filters is_active, and I have tests proving it. I
  added the explicit check anyway, with a test that pins it independently of the
  repository, because the security property shouldn't depend on a detail of
  another function.

  Next is sub-project 3, kiosks — registry, kiosk_type, onboarding, pricing,
  paper, assignments, and the refiller invite flow. It's the first module with
  real per-scope authorisation, so it's where kiosk_scope gets built and your
  "an owner controls only their own kiosks" rule becomes structural.

  The three most important have regression tests, and I checked those tests are
  load-bearing rather than decorative: reverting the harness recursion to the
  original buggy one-liner breaks 2 tests immediately, and restoring it goes
  back to 14 passing.

  That last check matters most, because defect #1 was a guardrail failing
  silently. A fix for a silent failure needs a test that fails loudly —
  otherwise you've just moved the silence.

  Full suite is green at 209 passing, 5 import contracts kept, ruff clean, and
  everything is committed (5c4306c is the tip). Nothing outstanding.

  One thing worth flagging honestly: these were all caught because something
  forced them into the open — the stale-entry test, ruff, a live server run. The
  seven I found are the ones that had a witness. That's the argument for the
  guardrails being in place before the kiosks module, where per-scope
  authorisation makes a silent failure much more expensive than a logged-out
  tab.

  - The pi-agent gets rewritten against /v1/device/* (your call, D11).
  - Frontend rewiring — 4 files in printvendo-web, 1 in printvendo-owner, 5 call
  sites in the refiller app.

  On "when"

  I won't invent a date — it depends on how much we do per session and how fast
  decisions come back. What I can say concretely:

  Nothing is deployable until sub-project 5 is done. A backend that can't take a
  payment can't run a kiosk. So the realistic first milestone is staging deploy
  after payments, running alongside production with migrated data, exercised by
  dev builds of your apps. Cutover comes after that proves out.

  The critical path runs through payments (5) and migration (8) — everything
  else can slip without moving the date. Both are blocked on things only you can
  provide.

  What I need from you, in priority order

  1. Razorpay test-mode key id + secret + webhook secret — blocks sub-project 5,
  which is the critical path
  2. A prod pg_dump (or SSH so I can take one) — blocks migration; also lets me
  validate assumptions about real data shapes early rather than at cutover
  3. Redis — winget install Redis or a container; blocks the device hub
  4. Who builds the admin console — me, or someone else against the API
  contract?

  None of those block kiosks, so I'm starting there now.

● I was wrong, and it's worth correcting because I built an argument on it.

  reset_printer_paper has exactly one implementation in services/printer_ops.py,
  called by kiosk.py, admin.py and refiller.py. Same for fail_active_jobs. The
  audit's "paper reset exists four times" describes a state that was since
  refactored away — I kept repeating it without re-checking.

  What is still duplicated is the route surface and its authorisation: three
  near-identical paper-reset endpoints across three routers, each re-deriving
  who's allowed. That's a real cost, but smaller than I claimed. Fixing the spec
  so the justification matches reality.

  The centrepiece is in and proven. Scope is a frozen dataclass with no default
  constructor — Scope() meaning "everything" is not expressible. Every
  repository read takes it as the first argument. I broke it three ways to check
  the tests bite:

  - make everyone unrestricted → 3 tests fail
  - drop the scope check in get → 2 fail
  - drop the scope filter in list → 2 fail

  Out-of-scope raises NotFound with byte-identical wording to a kiosk that
  doesn't exist, so an owner can't probe whether a competitor's kiosk id is
  real.

  Two things I fixed along the way that were my own errors: the test fixture
  only registered identity models, so kiosk tables existed by luck of import
  order (would have broken on any collection-order change); and I let a ruff
  failure through a commit because | tail -1 swallowed the exit code — caught
  and fixed in the next commit.

  On your timing question, restated with real numbers: kiosks is roughly half
  done in this session. At that rate the remaining six sub-projects are
  substantial but tractable. What actually moves your deployment date is the
  three unblocks — Razorpay test keys (critical path), a prod pg_dump, and
  Redis. Kiosks, ops and the API layers I can finish without you; payments and
  migration I cannot.

  Want me to keep going through kiosks 7–12, or would you rather I pause here
  and set up whichever of those three you can get me fastest?

  Two design points worth your attention:

  The billing stub fails closed. PlatformOnlyBilling answers "no" for every
  owner-gateway kiosk, so a SOLD/SAAS kiosk cannot go live until payments lands.
  That's deliberate — a stub answering "yes" would let a shop start taking
  student money with no verified route to its owner. It also means: until 
  sub-project 5, only PLATFORM kiosks can go live. Expected, not a gap.

  Lapse is now a state change, not just a gate. The old backend only guarded the
  promotion to LIVE, so a kiosk whose owner let their subscription lapse kept
  selling. reconcile_billing_state demotes LIVE → SUSPENDED_BILLING and restores
  it when billing is fixed. That's your D7 decision made structural.

  Also corrected a claim I'd been repeating: the old backend's paper logic is
  not duplicated four times — services/printer_ops.py was extracted at some
  point and all three routers call it. The audit doc I was citing is stale.
  What's actually duplicated is the route surface and its per-router
  authorisation. Spec updated so the justification matches reality.

  Blockers unchanged and still yours: Razorpay test keys (critical path), prod 
  pg_dump, Redis.

● Checked. Answering each.

  1. Trial + custom pricing — mostly exists, one real gap

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  What you want  │                        Status                         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                 │ Exists. Subscription.free_until — "treats the         │
  │ Trial access    │ subscription as active and unbilled until then".      │
  │ for SAAS owners │ Admin sets it via PUT                                 │
  │                 │ /owner/subscriptions/{user_id}/terms.                 │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Per-owner       │ Exists. Subscription.negotiated_price overrides the   │
  │ subscription    │ plan's monthly price, same endpoint.                  │
  │ price           │                                                       │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Discounts on    │ Exists but per-plan, not per-owner.                   │
  │ 6-month /       │ PlanDiscount(plan_id, duration_months, percent),      │
  │ annual packages │ admin-editable via PUT                                │
  │                 │ /owner/plans/{plan_id}/discounts.                     │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the genuine gap is: you can't currently give one specific owner a different
  annual discount from everyone else on that plan. You can change their monthly
  price, or change the discount for the whole plan — not one owner's duration
  discount.

  One thing this collides with, worth knowing now: a trial makes the 
  subscription count as active, which means the LIVE gate I just built will let
  a trialling SAAS kiosk go live. That's correct and intended — but it means
  free_until is a money-routing lever, not just a billing nicety.

  Recording both in the spec so sub-project 5 builds them.

● 2. What's left in identity — an honest list

  Identity is complete for what the plan covered. But comparing against what a
  real deployment needs, four gaps:

  Gap: No password reset / forgot password
  Severity: Real
  Note: A user who forgets their password has no path back. Worth knowing: the
  old backend doesn't have one either, so this isn't a regression — it's an
  existing hole you'd carry forward.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: No change-password while signed in
  Severity: Real
  Note: Standard expectation. Should also call revoke_all — "sign out
  everywhere".
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: revoke_all built but unwired
  Severity: Minor
  Note: Function exists and is tested; nothing calls it yet. Password change is
  its natural caller.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: No change-email
  Severity: Minor
  Note: Would need re-verification of the new address.

  Admin minting owner/refiller accounts is not a gap — it's correctly deferred
  to the admin API layer in sub-project 7.

  My recommendation: close password reset + change password now, while identity
  is fresh, rather than discovering it at cutover. It's ~2 tasks and reuses the
  verification-token machinery already built.

  3. How we continue

  Immediate order:
  1. Kiosks 10–12 — pricing (with the per-owner discount seam from D13),
  owner/refiller API layers, module docs
  2. Identity 11–12 — password reset + change password (the gaps above)
  3. Sub-project 4, printing — needs Redis
  4. Sub-project 5, payments + billing — needs Razorpay keys. Implements D13.
  Critical path.

  Continuing with kiosks 10 now — pricing, built so D13's per-owner overrides
  slot in without rework.

● My own tests caught a real semantic point. price_bw_double is the price of a
  sheet printed on both sides, not per side (per the old backend's comment:
  "first side = bw_single, second side = bw_double − bw_single"). So the
  invariant is right, and those two tests were setting an genuinely invalid
  state.

  That also implies an upper bound I'd missed: if double ≥ 2× single, a student
  is better off printing two single-sided sheets — duplex becomes a trap.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● 368 tests. Kiosks core logic complete — 10 of 12 tasks. Remaining there:
  owner/refiller API layers + module docs.

  Answering your three questions

  1. Trial + custom pricing. Three of four pieces already exist and carry over
  (free_until for trials, negotiated_price for per-owner rates, PlanDiscount for
  the 6/12-month ladder). The one genuine gap — per-owner duration discounts —
  is now recorded as spec decision D13 with the resolution order: owner override
  → plan default → none. It builds in sub-project 5.

  The non-obvious part I flagged in the spec: a trial is a money-routing lever.
  free_until makes a subscription count ACTIVE, which is exactly what the LIVE
  gate checks — so granting a SAAS owner a trial lets their kiosk go live and
  collect into their account, and trial expiry demotes it to SUSPENDED_BILLING
  just like a lapsed payment. That's the useful behaviour, but it means trials
  move real money.

  2. Identity gaps — four, one serious.

  No password reset. A user who forgets their password has no way back. Worth
  being precise: the old backend doesn't have one either, so this isn't a
  regression — it's a hole you'd carry into the new system. Also missing:
  change-password while signed in (should trigger the already-built-but-unwired
  revoke_all), and change-email.

  3. Order from here: kiosks 11–12 → identity password reset → printing (needs
  Redis) → payments/billing (needs Razorpay keys, implements D13, critical
  path).

  Two things worth noting from this stretch:

  My own tests caught a semantics error I'd have shipped. price_bw_double is per
  sheet printed both sides, not per side. That fixes it between bounds: below
  single-sided means paying less for more paper; above 2× means two single
  sheets are cheaper and duplex becomes a trap. I initially banned exactly-2×
  too, then relaxed it — same money, half the paper isn't a trap.

  The two placeholder seams fail in opposite directions, deliberately. Billing
  fails closed (no SOLD/SAAS kiosk can go live until payments lands — a
  misrouted payment is silent and irreversible). Price bands fail open (a silly
  price is visible and reversible). Both documented with the reasoning, so
  neither gets "fixed" to match the other.

  Continuing with kiosks 11 unless you'd rather I jump to the password reset gap
  first.

✻ Cogitated for 4m 47s

❯ do the passwords and continue                                                 

● Building it. First a decision worth stating: PasswordReset would be the third
  table with identical shape (token_hash, expires_at, used_at) after
  EmailVerification. Three copies of "hashed single-use expiring token" is
  exactly the drift this rewrite exists to stop — and each copy is a place to
  get the security properties subtly wrong.

  Unifying into one OneTimeToken with a purpose. Cheap now; nothing is in
  production.

● Passwords done. 402 tests, tree clean, committed (72bb156).

  Verified live, not just in tests: forgot-password returned byte-identical 
  responses for a real and a fake address, only the real one generated a token,
  old password rejected (401), new accepted (200), and the token refused on
  replay.

  The design call worth flagging: PasswordReset would have been the third table
  with identical shape after EmailVerification — three copies of "hashed,
  single-use, expiring token", three places to get single-use wrong, and a
  replay guard that ends up in only one. Merged into OneTimeToken with a purpose
  column. That bought a property I then tested explicitly: a verification link 
  cannot reset a password, and a reset link cannot verify an email. Without the
  purpose check, an email-confirmation link sitting in a mailbox would be an
  account takeover.

  Three other decisions:

  - Reset links live 1 hour, verification 24. A reset link is a credential —
  whoever holds it owns the account. Verification is a convenience.
  - Both reset and change revoke every session. The likeliest reason someone
  resets is that another person has the old password; leaving their refresh
  tokens alive hands the account back on the next rotation.
  - Change requires the current password. Otherwise a stolen 15-minute access
  token becomes permanent takeover.

  The migration is a drop-and-create, not a rename — I annotated it as
  destructive, because against a live database it would need a data-copying
  migration instead. Safe here only because nothing is deployed.

  Where everything stands

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        │                     Status                     │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Foundation             │ ✅                                             │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Identity               │ ✅ + password reset, change, verification      │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Kiosks                 │ 10/12 — core logic done, API layers + docs     │
  │                        │ left                                           │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Printing               │ needs Redis                                    │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Payments/billing       │ needs Razorpay keys — critical path,           │
  │                        │ implements D13                                 │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Ops, migration,        │ queued                                         │
  │ cutover                │                                                │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Next up is kiosks 11–12 (owner/refiller API layers, module docs), which
  finishes that module. Continuing unless you want something else first.

● Real bug, and a subtle one. The models declare Mapped[AssignmentRole] but the
  column is a plain String — SQLAlchemy doesn't coerce, so after a database
  round-trip you get a str, not the enum. Equality still works (they're
  StrEnum), which is why nothing caught it — but role.value raises
  AttributeError, and my new route does exactly that.

  Fixing at the source so the annotation is honest.

● Update(app\core\db.py)
  ⎿  Added 35 lines, removed 1 line
       6

       8  from contextlib import contextmanager
       9 +from enum import StrEnum                                       
      10  from functools import lru_cache
      11 +from typing import Any                                         
      12  
      11 -from sqlalchemy import Engine, create_engine                   
      13 +from sqlalchemy import Engine, String, TypeDecorator, create_en
         +gine                                                           
      14  from sqlalchemy.orm import DeclarativeBase, Session, sessionmak
          er
      15  
      16  
     ...
      18      pass
      19  
      20  
      21 +class EnumText(TypeDecorator):                                 
      22 +    """Store a StrEnum as text, and load it back *as the enum*.
      23 +                                                               
      24 +    Without this, a column annotated `Mapped[SomeEnum]` but typ
         +ed `String`                                                    
      25 +    returns a plain str after a database round-trip. Because th
         +ese are StrEnums                                               
      26 +    the deception is quiet: `value == SomeEnum.THING` still pas
         +ses, so tests go                                               
      27 +    green, while `value.value` raises AttributeError and `value
         + is SomeEnum.THING`                                            
      28 +    is False. The annotation should not lie.                   
      29 +                                                               
      30 +    Text rather than a native Postgres ENUM on purpose: adding 
         +a member to a                                                  
      31 +    native enum needs a migration and locks the type, which is 
         +a lot of ceremony                                              
      32 +    for what is a list of strings.                             
      33 +    """

● Kiosks module complete. 446 tests, 6 import contracts, 45 commits, tree clean.

  33 routes live, every one declared in the authorisation matrix.

  The one that matters: your rule — an owner controls only their own kiosks, 
  only admin controls all — is now structural. Scope is a frozen dataclass with
  no default constructor, so Scope() meaning "everything" isn't expressible.
  Every repository read takes it as the first argument; there is no unscoped
  read to forget. Admin is the same resolver with a wider scope, not a second
  router.

  Proven by breaking it: making every caller admin-scoped fails 9 HTTP-level 
  tests. Out-of-scope returns 404 with wording byte-identical to a kiosk that
  never existed — a 403 would confirm a competitor's kiosk is real.

  Two real bugs found in my own work this stretch:

  Enum columns were lying. Models declared Mapped[AssignmentRole] over a plain
  String. SQLAlchemy doesn't coerce, so after a DB round-trip you get a str.
  Because they're StrEnum, value == Enum.X still passes — every test stayed
  green — while value.value raises AttributeError. My new route called exactly
  that. Fixed with an EnumText decorator so the annotation is true, plus a test
  asserting identity and attribute access, the two things equality hides.

  Kiosks was reaching into identity's ORM in three files. I only found it
  because I added a contract to check — the existing independence contract
  listed one module, so it was checking nothing between them. Adding
  modules-do-not-reach-into-each-others-orm broke immediately and named both
  violations.

  That's the second time a guardrail turned out to be inspecting an empty set.
  Worth noting as a pattern: a passing contract proves nothing until you've
  watched it fail.

  Blockers unchanged — and now genuinely blocking, since kiosks is the last
  thing I can finish without you:

  1. Razorpay test keys — critical path
  2. Prod pg_dump — migration
  3. Redis — printing module

  Printing (sub-project 4) is next and needs Redis for the device WebSocket hub.
  I can build most of it against an in-process stub and swap later, or wait.
  Say which.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● All three are straightforward. WSL2 is enabled on your machine with no distro
  installed — that's the cleanest Redis path.

  ---
  1. Razorpay keys

  Don't paste them in chat. Open printvendo-backend\.env and fill these three
  lines:

  dotenv
  RAZORPAY_KEY_ID=rzp_test_xxxxxxxxxxxx
  RAZORPAY_KEY_SECRET=your_test_secret
  RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET=whatever_you_set_in_the_razorpay_dashboard

  .env is gitignored — I'll read it when I build the payments module. If you
  haven't created a webhook secret yet, invent any long random string now and
  set the same value in the Razorpay dashboard later.

  ---
  2. Production database dump

  ▎ Read this first. The dump contains every user's real data and — because of 
  ▎ Finding 1 in the spec — every kiosk owner's Razorpay secret in plaintext. 
  ▎ Keep it on your machine, don't put it in the repo, don't email it. Delete it
  ▎ once the migration is written and verified.

  Step 2a — on the VPS

  SSH in, then check whether last night's backup already exists (this avoids
  putting any load on the live database):

  ls -lh /opt/printit/backups/db-*.dump | tail -3

  If a recent dump is listed, skip to Step 2b.

  If nothing is listed, take one now:

  cd /opt/printit/cloud-backend/deploy
  source .env
  docker compose exec -T db pg_dump -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -Fc >
  /tmp/printit.dump
  ls -lh /tmp/printit.dump

  -Fc is the custom format your existing backup.sh already uses — compressed,
  and pg_restore can read it selectively.

  Step 2b — download to Windows

  In PowerShell on your machine, substituting your VPS user and host:

  scp USER@YOUR_VPS_HOST:/opt/printit/backups/db-*.dump
  "$env:USERPROFILE\Downloads\printit-prod.dump"

  (If you took a fresh one in Step 2a, the source path is /tmp/printit.dump
  instead.)

  Step 2c — restore into a local legacy database

  $env:PGPASSWORD = "postgres_local_dev"
  & "C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin\psql.exe" -U postgres -c "CREATE 
  DATABASE printit_legacy OWNER printvendo;"
  & "C:\Program Files\PostgreSQL\18\bin\pg_restore.exe" -U postgres -d
  printit_legacy --no-owner --no-privileges
  "$env:USERPROFILE\Downloads\printit-prod.dump"

  Some harmless warnings about roles or extensions are normal. Then tell me it's
  done — I'll verify row counts and start the migration mapping against real
  shapes rather than assumptions.

  Your production Postgres is 17 and local is 18; restoring into a newer major
  version is supported. The reverse would not be.

  ---
  3. Redis

  WSL2 is already enabled, just with no Linux installed. In an Administrator 
  PowerShell:

  wsl --install -d Ubuntu

  That reboots or drops you into Ubuntu setup — pick any username and password,
  they're local only. Then inside the Ubuntu window:

  sudo apt update && sudo apt install -y redis-server
  sudo sed -i 's/^appendonly no/appendonly yes/' /etc/redis/redis.conf
  sudo service redis-server start
  redis-cli ping

  PONG means done. WSL forwards localhost:6379 to Windows, so
  REDIS_URL=redis://localhost:6379/0 in .env already points at it — no change
  needed.

  To make it survive reboots, in the same Ubuntu window:

  echo 'sudo service redis-server start' >> ~/.bashrc

  ---
  Order of impact

  Razorpay first — it's the critical path and unblocks the biggest remaining
  sub-project. Dump second — I want to check real data shapes early rather than
  discover surprises at cutover. Redis last; it only gates the device hub, and I
  can build that module against a stub in the meantime.

  Do Razorpay and tell me — I'll start payments while you sort the other two.

● Your two-webhook setup surfaced something I need to flag before we go further.

  The old backend has only one RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET. Both handlers — payments
  and wallet top-up — verify against that same value via
  verify_platform_signature (services/razorpay_webhook.py:44). Razorpay lets
  each webhook have its own secret.

  And /wallet/topup/webhook is the only thing that credits a top-up.
  _credit_wallet_for_topup_order is called from exactly one place
  (wallet.py:757); there's no client-side verify endpoint for top-ups like there
  is for job payments.

  So: if your wallet webhook's secret differs from the payments one, every 
  wallet top-up in production is being taken and never credited. Signature check
  fails → 400 → balance never increases → student paid for nothing.

  Might be fine — you may have set both to the same string. Worth checking today
  either way.

  How to check, fastest first

  1. Razorpay dashboard → Settings → Webhooks. Compare the secret on both. If
  they differ, whichever doesn't match your server's .env is dead.

  2. Definitive — run this on the production database. It finds top-ups that
  were started and never credited:

  SELECT status, COUNT(*), MIN(created_at), MAX(created_at), SUM(amount)
  FROM wallet_ledger
  WHERE entry_type = 'TOPUP'
  GROUP BY status;

  On the VPS:

  cd /opt/printit/cloud-backend/deploy
  source .env
  docker compose exec -T db psql -U "$POSTGRES_USER" -d "$POSTGRES_DB" -c
  "SELECT status, COUNT(*), MIN(created_at), MAX(created_at), SUM(amount) FROM 
  wallet_ledger WHERE entry_type='TOPUP' GROUP BY status;"

  A pile of PENDING rows with recent timestamps means top-ups are not being
  credited. A handful of old ones is normal — those are abandoned checkouts.

  Answering what you asked

  For now, the payments secret in .env is the right choice — it's what
  production uses and what the job-payment flow depends on. Leave it.

  For the new backend this problem disappears by design: one webhook endpoint, 
  one secret (spec §7 — "One Razorpay webhook endpoint, not three. One URL to
  register."). At cutover you'll register a single webhook URL in Razorpay
  covering all events, and there will be exactly one secret that can be out of
  sync with nothing.

  Run the SQL and paste the output. If it shows stuck top-ups, that's a live
  money bug in the current system and worth fixing there directly — not waiting
  months for the rewrite.

● Keys are test mode. But RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET is a URL, not a secret — the
  value starts with https:// and ends in ook. You've pasted the webhook endpoint
  URL where the signing secret goes.

  In the Razorpay dashboard a webhook has three fields: URL, secret, and events.
  The secret is the separate one you type in (or generate) — it's what signs
  the X-Razorpay-Signature header. With a URL there, every webhook signature
  check fails.

  Fix printvendo-backend\.env:

  dotenv
  RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET=<the "Secret" field from Razorpay → Settings →
  Webhooks>

  If you never set one on the test webhook, type any long random string into
  Razorpay and put the same value here.

  Not blocking right now — the gate and billing don't touch webhooks. Starting
  there.

● Real problem, and it'll hit every future migration. Autogenerate emitted
  app.core.db.EnumText(length=16) into the migration without importing app —
  that's a NameError at runtime.

  The deeper issue: a migration must not depend on application code. Migrations
  are historical records; if EnumText is later renamed or moved, old migrations
  break. Teaching Alembic to render it as plain DDL instead.

● 466 tests. Payment configs done — and that's Finding 1 from the spec closed.

  The plaintext-secret defect is fixed and proven fixed. Owner Razorpay secrets
  are now Fernet ciphertext; the table has no plaintext column to write to; no
  API can return the secret (decrypt_secret is reachable only from the payment
  path). Three mutations confirm the tests bite:

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
  │            Broken             │ Tests failed │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Store the secret in plaintext │ 3            │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Let keys be replaced freely   │ 4            │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Never consume the approval    │ 2            │
  └───────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘

  I kept the old backend's set-once rule deliberately. It reads like bureaucracy
  but it's an anti-fraud control: without it, anyone who takes over an owner's
  account silently redirects every student payment at every one of their kiosks
  — and since owners are paid directly with no settlements, nothing would catch
  it.

  Also fixed a defect that would have broken every future migration.
  Autogenerate wrote app.core.db.EnumText(length=16) into the migration file
  without importing app — a NameError the moment it ran. Fixed with a
  render_item hook that renders custom types as plain sa.String.

  The deeper reason matters more than the bug: a migration must not depend on 
  application code. Migrations are historical records — if EnumText gets renamed
  next year, a migration from today has to keep working. I verified no
  migration in the tree references app.* at all now.

  One thing still needs you: RAZORPAY_WEBHOOK_SECRET in .env is a URL, not a
  secret. Not blocking yet — subscriptions and the gate don't touch webhooks —
  but it'll block the moment I wire payment verification.

  Next: plans and subscriptions, including your D13 (trials + per-owner duration
  discounts). Continuing.
