● Audit written to docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-15-legacy-data-audit.md.
  Findings:

  The reassuring part

  Structurally the database is fine. Zero orphaned jobs, payments, print jobs or
  ledger rows. Nothing corrupt, no dangling foreign keys. The data can be
  moved.

  What's actually wrong — all semantic

  1. Payment.status means more than the model says. The comment documents three
  values; production has six. CAPTURED isn't a Razorpay concept — it's what a
  wallet payment becomes once the print succeeds (pi.py:615).

  The problem isn't the extra value, it's that the codebase doesn't agree with 
  itself. kiosk.py counts PAID + CAPTURED in eight places. wallet.py,
  printers.py and pi.py filter on PAID alone. Same concept, two expressions, and
  roughly a sixth of successful payments are invisible to the second one. The
  wallet idempotency guard is one of them — once a payment turns CAPTURED, that
  guard stops seeing it.

  2. VIGNAN-1 XEROX is SOLD with zero owners. SOLD means the shop collects into
  their own Razorpay. With no owner linked, owner_of() returns None and the 
  platform takes the money — for a kiosk labelled as belonging to a shop.
  Settlements are retired, so nothing would ever catch it.

  3. Every kiosk is onboarding_stage = REGISTERED. All 21, including ones taking
  payments for months. The onboarding ladder and its LIVE gate were built and
  never used. That's also why finding 2 went unnoticed — the gate that would
  have caught it was never in the path.

  4. A few wallets disagree with their own ledger. The ledger has idempotency
  keys and unique constraints; the balance column doesn't. Migration should
  rebuild balances from ledgers, not copy the column.

  5. Paid-but-never-printed exists in production. Small rupee value, but it's
  the exact two-call wallet hazard the spec predicts — the clearest argument for
  the Order aggregate.

  6. REFUND_PENDING is written by one line and read by nothing. pi.py:355 marks
  them "for operator review"; no dashboard lists them. They just accumulate.
  That's real money owed to students.

  7. Case-duplicate emails — Person@ and person@ as separate accounts with
  separate wallets. These will collide with the new case-insensitive unique
  constraint.

  8. Noise: Dev-Printer-1 and TEST 1 are live kiosks; calcut and calicut look
  like the same shop typed twice; thousands of abandoned checkouts and
  payment-free guests.

  Three things need your decision, the rest is mechanical

  1. VIGNAN-1 XEROX — link an owner, or change its type?
  2. Case-duplicate accounts — merge, or keep one and deactivate? (Merge where
  either side holds a wallet balance; that's the student's money.)
  3. Test/duplicate kiosks — which to quarantine? Can't just delete ones with
  real payments.

  Nothing here is urgent — none of it blocks building. Answer when convenient
  and I'll bake the rules into the migration.

  Continuing with plans and subscriptions.

● 455 people hold ₹7,264.50

  (as of that dump — not current)

  ┌──────────┬────────┬───────────┐
  │ Balance  │ People │   Total   │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ Under ₹5 │ 139    │ ₹334.50   │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ₹5–19    │ 199    │ ₹1,832.50 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ₹20–49   │ 86     │ ₹2,758.00 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ₹50–99   │ 25     │ ₹1,481.00 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ₹100+    │ 6      │ ₹858.50   │
  └──────────┴────────┴───────────┘

  Average ₹15.97, largest ₹294. Out of 3,131 wallet rows total, so most are
  empty.

  Caveat on that number. Three wallets disagree with their own ledger. Two are
  test accounts. The third is real — msrihari@gmail.com, and it's the largest 
  balance in the system:

  - 22 debits, ₹200 spent
  - 6 top-ups totalling ₹310, all stuck PENDING
  - stored balance ₹294

  Even counting the pending top-ups, the arithmetic gives ₹104, not ₹294. So
  ₹500 entered that balance with no ledger row at all — an admin adjustment or a
  direct database edit. Not fraud-shaped, but it means the balance column has
  been written to outside the ledger, which is why the migration should rebuild
  balances from ledgers rather than trust the column.

  The bigger number, and why it's probably fine

  330 top-ups sit PENDING, worth ₹15,378 — more than twice the live balance.
  That looked alarming, so I checked whether it's money taken and not credited.

  It almost certainly isn't:

  - Zero PENDING rows have a razorpay_payment_id. That field only appears once
  Razorpay has actually captured money. No captured payment is attached to any
  of them.
  - SUCCESS rows run right through to the last day in the dump. If the webhook
  were broken, successes would have stopped on some date. They didn't — so the
  webhook works.

  Reads as ordinary checkout abandonment: 784 completed, 330 abandoned, ~30%.
  Normal for a payment flow.

  I can't prove it from the database alone, though. A student could have paid
  with the webhook failing, leaving no payment id either way. The definitive 
  check is Razorpay's dashboard — compare captured payments against the 784
  SUCCESS rows. If those match, nothing is lost.

  What it means for cutover

  ₹7,264 across 455 students is a real liability you're carrying into the new 
  system — they paid for it. It's small enough to honour outright, which is what
  the migration does: balances rebuilt from ledgers, every discrepancy reported
  with its delta rather than silently corrected.

  Worth telling those 455 people during the maintenance window that their
  balance carries over. It's the kind of thing that costs nothing to say and is
  very expensive to get wrong quietly.

● Your audit findings are design requirements. Let me close the first one

  users.email has unique=True, which in Postgres is case-sensitive. Person@x.com

  registration catches it — but the database doesn't, so a race or any direct
  insert reproduces exactly the 10 duplicates you have in production.

● 483 tests. Your requirement is now a checkable table in the spec, not a wish.
  Every audit finding maps to a mechanism:

  ┌─────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬──────────────────┐

  │ PAID vs CAPTURED            │ One status enum, one   │ sub-project 5    │
  │ split-brain                 │ success predicate      │                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Balance disagrees with      │ Ledger is the record;  │                  │
  │ ledger                      │ balance derived, never │ sub-project 5    │
  │                             │  written independently │                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │                             │ Order aggregate — one  │                  │
  │ Paid-but-never-printed      │ transaction, state     │ sub-project 5    │
  │                             │ unreachable            │                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ REFUND_PENDING written by   │ Refunds get an owner   │ sub-projects 5–6 │
  │ one line, read by nothing   │ and a worklist         │                  │
  └─────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  I found the same email bug in my own new schema. unique=True on email is
  case-sensitive in Postgres — Person@x.com and person@x.com would both insert,
  exactly reproducing your 10 production duplicates. The repository lowercased
  in Python, but that can't hold: two concurrent registrations both pass the
  check and both insert. Now enforced by a functional unique index on
  lower(email), and removing it fails a test.

  That's the pattern behind every finding in your audit: a rule that lived in 
  one place and wasn't enforced in another. A check in Python but not the
  database. A status written by one router and read differently by the next. A
  gate that was built but never put in the path. The answer isn't more care —
  it's one implementation per rule, with something mechanical that fails when
  bypassed.

  Billing is in, including your D13 request. Pricing resolves in one function
  with a documented chain — negotiated_price → plan rate, then owner discount →

  Three things worth calling out.

  Your VIGNAN-1 XEROX case is now structurally impossible — a SOLD kiosk with no
  owner returns CLOSED, so it cannot take a rupee rather than quietly routing
  to the platform. There's a test named for exactly that scenario.

  A lapse never falls back to the platform. That's deliberate and it's the more
  expensive-looking choice: the shop goes dark. But the alternative is holding a
  shop's takings with no settlement mechanism to return them — settlements are
  retired, so that money would have no correct destination. Visible failure
  beats silent debt.

  The accepts_wallet flag can only restrict, never permit. Even if someone
  forces it true on a SOLD kiosk, wallet_may_be_spent still says no, because the
  gate isn't PLATFORM_GATEWAY. That's the original leak's exact shape —
  Printvendo keeps the top-up cash while the owner prints for free — and it's
  now unreachable through the flag.

  Also: the LIVE gate and the payment path are now literally the same function.
  They couldn't disagree even by accident, so a kiosk can't sit LIVE while
  unable to take money.

● Webhook secret is fixed — 35 chars, not a URL.

  On WSL: don't fight it. You probably don't need Redis locally at all.

  The stuck loading bar is almost always the Microsoft Store fetch. Bypass it
  with:

  wsl --install -d Ubuntu --web-download

  But before you bother — here's why I'd skip it:

  Redis exists in this design for exactly one thing: the device WebSocket 
  registry, so production can run more than one uvicorn worker. That's what the
  old backend could never do (ws_manager held sockets in a per-process dict).

  Locally you run one worker, so Redis buys you nothing. And on the VPS it runs
  as a container in the compose stack — already in the deploy plan.

  For tests I'll use fakeredis: a real in-memory implementation of the Redis
  protocol, written for exactly this. Tests get faster and don't depend on an
  external service, which also helps CI.

  The honest caveat: fakeredis isn't Redis. Pub/sub semantics are close but not
  identical, so the hub gets exercised against real Redis at the staging deploy,
  not before. I'll note that in the plan so it's a known checkpoint rather than
  a surprise.

  So: leave WSL alone. If you'd rather have real Redis locally anyway,
  --web-download is the fix, or Memurai is a native Windows Redis-compatible
  service. Neither is needed.

  Starting the printing module — documents and the PDF pipeline, which need
  neither Redis nor Razorpay.

❯ papers should not waste , paper count should be accurate                      

● Two different things there, and the second one is where the old system
  drifted.

  On "not wasting": the per-copy rounding is correct, not waste. Two copies of a
  5-page duplex document is 6 sheets, not 5. If they shared, copy one's last
  page would land on the same sheet as copy two's first page — you couldn't hand
  either to anyone. CUPS starts each copy on a fresh sheet for the same reason,
  so 6 is what the printer actually uses.

  On accuracy: my sheets number is a prediction. Reality is whatever CUPS does.
  The old backend only ever used its estimate, and only ever deducted on PRINTED
  — so a job that jammed after three sheets deducted zero, and the tray count
  drifted from the real tray. That's why kiosks show paper remaining when
  they're empty.

  Fixing both in the design.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● 545 tests. Paper accuracy is done properly.

  What I found in the old print path

  I read the agent and the server before designing anything. Three real defects,
  and I found the cause of your duplicate-print problem.

  The duplicate print. GET /pi/{printer_id}/jobs/next selects the oldest queued
  job, returns it, and never changes its status. The agent calls that endpoint
  from two places — the main loop and a prefetch worker — and the status only
  becomes QUEUED_ON_PI after it decides to print. Two fetches in that window get
  the same job and both print it. Same hole reopens on restart: an agent that
  prints then crashes before its status call reprints on the next poll.

  Four independent readings of one options blob. Job.options is JSON parsed with
  its own try/except and its own defaults in four places — pricing, storage,
  paper accounting, and the agent's CUPS command. Nothing forces them to agree,
  so what you charge, what you deduct, and what prints are three separate
  opinions.

  Paper drift. The old code deducted its estimate, and only on PRINTED. A job
  that jammed after three sheets deducted zero. That's why a kiosk reports paper
  while being empty.

  What's built

  One options calculation. pages / impressions / sheets, derived once, used by
  pricing, paper and the device. Five mutations tried, all caught — including
  "copies share a sheet" and "duplex ignored for paper".

  Paper from reality, not guesswork. The device reports what CUPS actually
  pulled; that wins over the prediction. A failed print deducts what it managed.
  Mismatches get written to the refill log — a printer consistently
  overshooting is telling you its driver is ignoring duplex.

  On your "no waste" point: two copies of a 5-page duplex document is 6 sheets, 
  not 5 — and that's correct, not waste. Sharing would put copy one's last page
  on the same sheet as copy two's first, so neither could be handed to anyone.
  CUPS does the same.

  One weak test caught and fixed. My clamp test asserted through
  sheets_remaining, which already does max(0, …) — so it passed even with the
  clamp deleted. Now asserts on used directly, plus a test showing the
  consequence: an unclamped used breaks the next refill, which computes from
  capacity minus used.

  Next is the atomic claim — FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED — which makes the duplicate
  print structurally impossible rather than unlikely.

✻ Crunched for 11m 13s

❯ what ?                                                                        

● Sorry — that was too dense. Here's the important bit plainly.

  Why your printer sometimes prints the same thing twice:

  When a Pi asks the server "give me the next job to print", the server hands
  one over — but never marks it as taken. The job stays sitting in the queue
  looking unclaimed.

  Your Pi agent asks for a job from two places at once (a main loop and a
  background pre-fetcher, so the next file downloads while the current one
  prints). Because the server never marked the first job as taken, both of them
  can be handed the same job — and both print it.

  Same thing happens if the Pi loses power mid-print. The job was never marked
  as taken, so when it comes back it prints it again.

  The fix is to make the server mark a job as claimed in the same instant it
  hands it over, so a second request physically cannot receive the same one.
  That's the next thing I'm building.

  Two other things I found and already fixed:

  - The paper counter only decreased when a print fully succeeded. If a print
  jammed halfway, it subtracted nothing — so the system thinks there's paper
  when the tray is empty. Now it subtracts what the printer actually used, even
  on failure.
  - Your print settings (colour, double-sided, page range, copies) were being
  read separately in four different places in the code — one for pricing, one
  for the printer, one for paper counting, one for saving. Nothing kept them in
  agreement, so you could be charged for one thing and get another. Now there's
  one calculation everything reads.

  Which part do you want me to go deeper on — or shall I just keep building the
  fix?

✻ Brewed for 17s

❯ Next is the atomic claim — FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED — which makes the duplicate 
  print structurally impossible rather than unlikely. what is this , and        
  continue working                                                              

● Plain version: it's a way to hand out work so two workers can never grab the
  same item.

  Think of a queue of jobs as a stack of paper slips. Right now the server reads
  the top slip and passes a photocopy — the original stays on the pile, so the
  next person reads the same one.

  FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED makes the database physically take the slip off the 
  pile in the same instant it hands it over. The word "SKIP" is the important
  part: if a second request arrives while the first is being served, it doesn't
  wait or error — it just moves to the next slip. Postgres guarantees this at
  the database level, so it holds no matter how many Pis, threads or prefetchers
  are asking.

  It's the standard way to build a job queue on Postgres. Building it now.

● 15 pass; the two concurrency tests fail — and the reason is instructive. The
  db_session fixture wraps everything in an outer transaction that's rolled
  back, so its commit() never makes rows visible to other connections. A real
  concurrency test needs really-committed data.

● Answering your question properly

  A fresh session would now be in decent shape — but you were right to ask, 
  because two real gaps existed and I've just closed them.

  When you asked, CLAUDE.md still said "446 tests, printing not yet built" while
  printing was half-built at 562 tests. And a test was failing — printing
  models existed with no migration. A new session reading that doc would have
  started from a false picture. Both fixed and committed.

  What now carries across reliably:

  - The spec's §2a table — every legacy defect mapped to the mechanism
  preventing it, so the why survives without me
  - The data audit, with the three decisions still waiting on you
  - Each plan's outcome section listing defects found while building it
  - 562 tests, which pin behaviour far better than prose
  - Docstrings that say why, not what — "this fails closed because a misrouted
  payment is silent and irreversible" is the kind of thing that stops a later
  change undoing a deliberate choice

  What I've added specifically for a cold start: a "How this work is done"
  section at the top of CLAUDE.md — TDD, mutation-test anything that matters,
  one rule/one mechanism, verify don't assume. Plus an honest state-of-play with
  what's built, what's next in dependency order, and what's blocked on you.

  Where a new session could still drift, honestly:

  - It won't automatically mutation-test unless it reads and follows that
  section
  - It might repeat a stale claim from an old doc rather than checking the
  source — I did exactly that with the "paper reset exists four times" line
  until I re-read the code
  - Judgement calls made in conversation but never written down are simply gone

  So: it'll follow the path, but it won't reproduce the scepticism unless 
  prompted. If you start fresh, saying "read CLAUDE.md and continue the printing
  module" will get you much further than "continue the backend".

  Meanwhile — the duplicate print is fixed

  Two genuinely concurrent Postgres transactions now race for the same task and
  only one can win. Removing SKIP LOCKED makes the test hang rather than pass,
  which is the proof: the second caller blocks instead of skipping. Your old
  backend didn't block — it just handed out the same job twice.

  Also fixed a weak test of my own on the way: my first concurrency test used
  the standard db_session fixture, which runs inside a rolled-back outer
  transaction — so its "committed" rows were invisible to the second connection
  and the test couldn't have detected anything. It now uses really-committed
  data.
